### R22. `records fetched` after an early close, and the agent's own failure records (measurement 3f)

**Measured** (4.0.7 and 5.0.4, both modes, the connector's own wire client):
- After answering an `op_fetch`, the server fetches rows of the cursor ahead. Every full-read, `Flow::Stop` (byte budget) and row-limit case fits **requested ≤ `records fetched` ≤ requested + 64** (requested: the sum of the row counts of the connector's `op_fetch` messages), capped by the end of the result; measured from the rows the connector **counted**, the record exceeded them by up to 127 (`Flow::Stop`), 65 (the row limit) and 128 (a client deadline). R13 item 3's "between the counted rows and the counted rows plus 64" does not hold.
- The read-ahead is not the size of the **last** request: with a row limit of 64 the connector asks for 64 rows, then 1 (the limit plus one), and the record says 129 (requested 65 plus 64); with a limit of 100, 64 then 37, and 165 (101 plus 64). With a limit of 1 or 10 (single requests of 2 and 11 rows), 4 and 22. Every value fits **requested plus the largest request of the execution**.
- A client deadline in the middle of a multi-round fetch ended with the attachment dropped. The server wrote nothing at the deadline; the statement finish record, `ROLLBACK_TRANSACTION` and `DETACH_DATABASE` came 5 to 6.5 s later, when the server noticed the closed connection. In the connector on `dev` (`conn.rs`, `fetch_all` and `after_client_failure`), the deadline is checked only while the connector waits on the socket, so a deadline that passes in the row callback is noticed after one more `op_fetch` has been sent; the connector then sends `op_cancel`, and drops the attachment when no answer comes within its 5 s cancel wait. That reading of the code is consistent with the measured delay but was not traced on the wire; the connector lot confirms it (measurement 3i).
- The server's statement timeout during a fetch writes `FAILED EXECUTE_STATEMENT_FINISH` with `0 records fetched` and a block (the read table), then `ERROR AT JResultSet::fetchNext` with `335544794` (`isc_cancelled`) and `335545129` (statement timeout).
- After the finish record of a row-limit read whose last request reached the end of the result, the server wrote `ERROR AT JResultSet::fetchNext` with `335544364` (`isc_req_sync`, request synchronization error) on the agent's attachment, in every run, although the connector saw no error.
- R13's own-statement table has no entry for either `ERROR` record or for a `FAILED` finish.

**Proposed**, replacing R13 item 3's tolerance; R8 item 4's other conditions (the own-attachment rule, the closed statement set, one execution credit per execution) stay:

1. **What a credit records.** For each execution the connector records, besides the attachment, text and transaction: the rows it **counted** (C), the rows it **requested** (Q, the sum of the row counts of the `op_fetch` messages it wrote to the socket for that execution), the size of its last request (L), the size of its **largest** request (M, at most 64, decision 1), and how the fetch ended:
   - **end**: the server's end-of-result answer was read;
   - **early**: the connector stopped before the end (the byte budget, the row-limit cut, or a client deadline after which the answer to every request it sent was read, item 3);
   - **lost**: the attachment was dropped while a request's answer was still unread;
   - **failed**: the statement ended with the server's statement timeout (the connector read `isc_cancelled` and the timeout code);
   - **error**: any other server error.
2. **The bound on `records fetched`** (N) of the matching record:
   - **end**: N = C, as R13 item 3 says.
   - **early**: Q ≤ N ≤ Q + M. Every request whose answer the connector read before stopping was answered in full (a shorter answer would have carried the end of the result), so the server fetched at least Q rows; its read-ahead adds at most the largest request. The review's L1 proposed Q + L (the last request); that bound is **not** used, because the measured row-limit cut breaks it (64 → 129 with Q = 65, L = 1): Q + M is the tightest bound that fits every measured value, and is never wider than Q + 64.
   - **lost**: Q − L ≤ N ≤ Q + M: the last request may or may not have reached the server.
   - **failed**: 0 ≤ N ≤ Q + M (the statement timeout wrote 0).

   The table's static bound (1 for own-row reads and probes, the setting count for the `RDB$CONFIG` read, the connector's row limit for a catalog read, decisions 4 and 5) stays an outer bound.
3. **A connector change for lot 3: the client deadline in a multi-round fetch** (decision 3, "Statement timeout"). Proposed because it makes the record of an interrupted read arrive at once, keeps the attachment usable, and leaves the wider `lost` bound for the rare case where the server does not answer:
   - the fetch loop checks the client deadline **before each `op_fetch`**; past it, it sends no further `op_fetch` and closes the cursor with `DSQL_drop`, as the byte-budget path already does (lot 2 implementation note, review L1): no request is pending, so an `op_cancel` could only reach the next one. The fetch ends **early**;
   - when the deadline passes while an `op_fetch` is pending, the connector sends `op_cancel` and reads until the pending request's answer is complete (rows read, dropped and zeroized) or the cancelled answer arrives (obligation 2: `isc_cancelled` within 0 to 2 ms), then `DSQL_drop`. Either answer shows that the request reached the server: **early**;
   - **a stale cancel** (security review L2): when the pending fetch was answered by rows or its end rather than by `isc_cancelled`, the `op_cancel` may still reach the next request, the `DSQL_drop`. An `isc_cancelled` answer to that `DSQL_drop` is then expected: the connector records it on the same credit as a **stale-cancel follow-up** (item 6), and the attachment stays usable. Any other answer, or none within the 5 s cancel wait, drops the attachment (**lost**). **When that `DSQL_drop` succeeds** (security review L7), the cancel may still be pending on the server and reach a later request. Until measurement 3i shows whether it can, the connector **ends the session cleanly** (rolls back, detaches and reconnects for its next statement), so no later request of that attachment can meet it; if 3i shows that a cancel cannot outlive the answered fetch, the connector keeps the attachment. The other option, carrying the stale-cancel follow-up to the next request of that attachment, is the fallback if a clean end proves costly; 3i decides, in an implementation note;
   - only when neither answer arrives within the 5 s cancel wait is the attachment dropped, as today (**lost**), with the lifetime rule of decision 3 for a lost connection. **That lifetime** (security review N5) is sized from measurement 3i: decision 3's "lost plus the statement timeout plus 5 s" must cover the delay between the drop and the server's records of it (5 to 6.5 s measured here); if 3i measures more, the margin is raised to the measured maximum plus 5 s, in an implementation note before the Audit lot merges.

   The records the server writes for these paths are measured in the connector lot (3i) and recorded in the own-statement table before the Audit lot merges.
4. **Which rule applies before 3i** (security review L1). A credit that ended **early** expects a plain `EXECUTE_STATEMENT_FINISH`. A `FAILED EXECUTE_STATEMENT_FINISH` matching an **early** credit (for example after the connector's own cancel) is **not** the agent's own until 3i records that form in the own-statement table: it is handled as any client's record (its block's relations reported, R8 item 5). Item 5 applies only to a credit that ended **failed**. A credit that ended **error** (any other server error) gives no own record and no follow-up: its records are not the agent's own (fail closed).
5. **The server's statement timeout on the agent's attachment.** A `FAILED EXECUTE_STATEMENT_FINISH` is the agent's own when R8 item 4 holds with these trailer conditions: its credit ended **failed**; its block, present or absent, names no relation outside the text's may-read set plus its metadata-loading set (R23; for a sampling text, the sampled table only; for the CAS guard's text, the ticket registry only); and N is within the **failed** bound. The credit then holds one **error follow-up**: the next record of that binding may be an `ERROR` record whose GDS codes are exactly `335544794` and `335545129`; it consumes the follow-up and produces no event.
6. **The request synchronization error, and the stale cancel.** A credit that ended **early** by the row-limit cut holds one error follow-up for an `ERROR` record whose only GDS code is `335544364`, as the next record of that binding after the credited finish record. A credit with a stale-cancel follow-up (item 3) holds one for an `ERROR` record whose only GDS code is `335544794`. Each consumes its follow-up and produces no event. The cause of the synchronization error is recorded by 3i; if the connector lot removes it (for example by not asking for one row beyond the limit when the end of the result can be reached), that follow-up is withdrawn in an implementation note.
7. **The shape of a follow-up `ERROR` record** (security review L2) is exact and closed, per version, from the matrix table (3i): the header, the attachment line, the client line where the version writes one, the transaction line, the location line (`JResultSet::fetchNext` or the location the table records for the path), and one line per expected GDS code, nothing else. A record with any other line, or with a trailer under it (`records fetched`, a performance line, a block), does not have the shape and is **ambiguous** (H1 fix 1: a trailer that does not fit the event).
8. **Never hiding a user read.** An `ERROR` record of the follow-up shape carries no statement text, no block and no row count, so consuming one hides nothing. An own-attachment `ERROR` record that matches no unconsumed follow-up is handled as any client's `ERROR` record (decision 6: GDS codes kept, texts dropped), never as the agent's own; one that has the shape of a follow-up when the credit's follow-up is already consumed is **ambiguous**, as R8 item 4 treats a statement record with no credit left (a forged copy is loud). A `FAILED` finish whose block names a relation outside the may-read set is not the agent's own (R8 item 5): its read is reported.

**Alternatives considered.** (a) Keep "counted plus 64" and widen it to "counted plus 128": it ties the bound to rows the server never saw. (b) Accept the measured deadline path without a connector change: every client deadline in a multi-round fetch would use the **lost** bound and leave the record 5 s or more after the deadline. (c) Report the two `ERROR` kinds as the agent's ordinary error records: they would appear on the console as failures of the agent's account at every statement timeout and every cut read. (d) Drop the attachment on any stale cancel: simpler, but every deadline that races a fetch answer would use the **lost** path.

**Residual risks.** Under **lost**, a forged copy of an agent record that has guessed the token can carry up to M more rows than under **early**, of the relations the text may read (R13's analysis, unchanged otherwise; consuming the credit makes the real record ambiguous).

**Effect.** No change to levels, notes or protocol. One connector change for lot 3 (item 3), with tests against the fake server and the engine matrix.

### R23. Per-text metadata-loading sets, and the lot 3 texts (measurement 3d of the lot 3 texts)

**Measured**: the candidate `RDB$CONFIG` read of R3, as `ext` and `SYSDBA`, names **`RDB$CHARACTER_SETS` and `RDB$COLLATIONS`** (1 indexed read each) in the block of the first read on an attachment that returns rows, never `RDB$CONFIG` (a virtual relation, R8), whatever the cast and also as the attachment's first `MON$` read; later reads on the same attachment are block-less; the minimal account reads 0 rows, block-less. The read-back probe of proof 7 (`RDB$DATABASE`, 1 row, every account and position), the `MON$SEC_DATABASE` candidate (block-less after the first `MON$` read, the metadata-loading set as the first) and the two view-dependency candidates (`RDB$VIEW_RELATIONS`, `RDB$DEPENDENCIES`: a block naming their relation, `records fetched` the matching rows) behave as R13 expects. R13 item 2 allows the metadata-loading set (`RDB$DATABASE`, `RDB$RELATIONS`) only for `MON$` / `SEC$` texts, so it would reject the agent's own `RDB$CONFIG` read.

**Proposed**, amending R13 items 1 and 2:

1. **A per-text metadata-loading set.** Each row of the own-statement table (R13 item 1, R20) records its own metadata-loading set, per version, measured by the 3d matrix test: `RDB$DATABASE` and `RDB$RELATIONS` for a text that reads a `MON$` or `SEC$` relation (as R13 says), `RDB$CHARACTER_SETS` and `RDB$COLLATIONS` for the `RDB$CONFIG` read, and the empty set otherwise unless the test records one. The set is allowed on any execution of its text, for R13's reason (only the first was measured, and allowing it later adds only system relations). The table test asserts that every relation of such a set is a system relation of the never-sampled list (decision 5, lot 2).
2. **Must-read sets that depend on the counted rows.** For a text that reads one persistent catalog relation (the two view-dependency texts), the relation is in the must-read set of an execution whose credit counted at least one row; when the credit counted none, the block **names that relation or is absent** (security review N1: a scan that reads records and matches none keeps its block, R12; an empty relation writes none). For the `RDB$CONFIG` read, the must-read set is empty (a virtual relation never appears in a block) and the may-read set is `RDB$CONFIG`.
3. **The rows of the lot 3 texts** join the table with the pull request that adds each text to `sql::STATEMENTS` (R20), as measured here: the read-back probe (must-read and may-read `RDB$DATABASE`, 1 row); `MON$SEC_DATABASE` (empty must-read set, the `MON$` metadata-loading set, 1 row); `RDB$CONFIG` (item 2, at most the six settings of its text, R3); `RDB$VIEW_RELATIONS` and `RDB$DEPENDENCIES` (item 2, the connector's row limit for those reads, R12 item 5); `RDB$LINGER` (R30), measured when it is added.

**Forging.** A forged copy of the `RDB$CONFIG` read may now carry two more system relations, both noise for every other client: nothing beyond R13's analysis and its L2 residual.

**Alternatives considered.** (a) Add `RDB$CHARACTER_SETS` and `RDB$COLLATIONS` to the one shared metadata-loading set: every `MON$` / `SEC$` text would then accept them, which no measurement shows. (b) Allow any system relation in an own block: rejected in R13 (alternative (b)).

**Effect.** No change to levels, notes or protocol.

### R24. Blocks on transaction-end records and on transaction statements (measurement 3g)

**Measured**:
- After DDL, `COMMIT_TRANSACTION` carries a **block** of system relations after its performance line (`RDB$DATABASE`, `RDB$FIELDS`, `RDB$RELATIONS`, `RDB$RELATION_FIELDS`, `RDB$SECURITY_CLASSES`, `RDB$USER_PRIVILEGES` for `CREATE TABLE`; `RDB$SECURITY_CLASSES` alone for `CREATE SEQUENCE`). R20 says a transaction-end record's expected trailer is a performance line.
- On a database whose transaction triggers read a table, `isql`'s `SET TRANSACTION`, `COMMIT` and `ROLLBACK` statement records (no `Statement <n>:` line, `0 records fetched`) carry the **block of the transaction trigger they fired**, which repeats the trigger record's block (R15 already reports it). R20 and R8 case (b) require "exactly `0 records fetched`, no parameter line and no block", so these records are ambiguous.

**Proposed**, amending R20 ("Transaction-end records", "A statement finish without a `Statement <n>:` line", "R8 case (b) itself") and H1 fix 1's expected trailer shapes:

1. **Transaction-end records** (`COMMIT_TRANSACTION`, `ROLLBACK_TRANSACTION`) of a binding other than an internal or sweep binding: the expected trailer is the performance line, then a block or none. A transaction-end record without a performance line stays ambiguous (R20). The internal shape (R16 item 1: a performance line on `COMMIT_TRANSACTION`, no block) and the sweep sequence (R17) are unchanged: a block there is ambiguous.
2. **What a block on a transaction-end record becomes.** An access event of the binding's principal (the attachment's user and role) with the relations of the block under the filters, no `rows` (the record has no row count) and no signal; a block with no relation left after the filters produces no event. **System relations outside the always-reported lists are noise here, whatever their counters** (security review L3): the DDL that changed them is reported by its own statement record and kind (decision 6: DDL keeps its kind), and the server refuses user DML on system tables since Firebird 3.0 (to confirm by a matrix test, measurement 3k), so such a relation in a transaction-end block comes from DDL or from the server's own work. **Relations on the always-reported lists** (statement-text and credential-bearing, decision 6, and views over them, R12 item 5) keep their name and the always-reported mark. A user relation in such a block (for example a commit trigger's read, if a version writes it there) is reported. Never the agent's own when a relation is left after the filters (trigger code runs with the agent's rights, R15 item 4).
3. **Transaction statements.** A statement finish of kind `SET TRANSACTION`, `COMMIT` or `ROLLBACK` (with or without a `Statement <n>:` line), with exactly `0 records fetched` and no parameter line, **may carry a block**; it is then well formed (no split window) and gives an event as in item 2 (the block's relations under the filters, no `rows`). Every other kind of R8 case (b) (`ALTER SESSION RESET`, `SAVEPOINT`, `SET BIND` and the other session-management statements) keeps "no block": a block there is ambiguous, until a measurement shows that a kind fires a trigger (3k: `ALTER SESSION RESET` on a database with connect and disconnect triggers).
4. **The duplicate.** The event of item 3 repeats the relations of the transaction trigger's own event (R15 item 4). It is kept, not folded: folding would need a cross-record match on a rule that R15 item 3 keeps out ("never folded"), and a duplicate read with no row count is noise, not a hidden read.

**Forging.** A forged transaction-end or transaction-statement header can capture a real trailer only when that trailer fits: a real statement trailer has `records fetched`, which a transaction-end record does not have (ambiguous); a real statement trailer with `0 records fetched` and a block (a scan that matched nothing) fits a forged transaction statement, and a real routine trailer (a performance line and a block) fits a forged transaction-end record. In both cases the block's user relations, and its always-reported relations with their mark, are reported under the forged binding's principal: the accepted misattribution residual (refinement of 2026-10-25, open question 6), with no row count lost (the captured trailer has none, or 0).

**Alternatives considered.** (a) Keep R20: every DDL commit and every transaction statement on a database with transaction triggers opens a split window. (b) No event for blocks on transaction records: a forged transaction header could then drop a captured routine block. (c) Fold the transaction statement's block into the trigger's event: see item 4.

**Effect.** No change to levels, notes or protocol.

### R25. Block-ful per-row routine records, pending groups and the hold bound (measurement 3g)

**Measured**: a statement that runs a routine per row writes one nested record per row: **10 000** before each statement finish of a 10 000-row `INSERT … SELECT` or `SELECT F(x) FROM big`, all on the statement's attachment and transaction, all followed by it. Block-less for a trigger that sets an id and a function that reads nothing; **block-ful** for a trigger and a function that read a table (`CUSTOMERS`, 3 natural reads each), whose statement's block holds the **sum** (`CUSTOMERS` 30 000). Records of three other attachments written between the first nested record and the statement finish: 9 to 27 (6 to 19 KiB) in most cases, **203 (139 KiB) once**, within 79 ms (4.0.7 SuperServer).

R14 item 4 counts only block-less pending records, so block-ful ones would be held as records, in the same numbers. R8 item 2's hold bound of 256 records of other bindings was nearly reached by three ordinary sessions during one short statement; under load, a per-row statement would reach it and turn ambiguous.

**Proposed**, amending R4 (folding by maximum), R14 item 4 and R8 item 2:

1. **A pending group per binding and transaction**, for every nested record (block-less or block-ful) waiting for its statement finish: the count of its records; the position of its first record **and, separately, the position of its first block-less record** (security review M1), if any; the union of the routine names (R4's folded object names), at most 256; the union of the relations of their blocks with, **per relation, the maximum of each of the eight counters**, at most 256 relations; and the largest `records fetched` among them (selectable procedures, R14). Not the records themselves: each is parsed, merged into the group and its buffer zeroized.
2. **Folding.** At the next statement finish of the same binding and transaction (under R4's conditions), the group folds as R4 says: objects are the union of the statement's and the group's, each relation's counters and the row count are the maximum of the statement's and the group's. **The per-relation maximum is kept, rather than relying on the statement's block alone** (which holds the sum): N-1 of the review of 2026-10-25 showed that a forged statement finish placed after real nested records must not be able to drop their relations or lower their counts, and the maximum gives that with state bounded by the number of relations, not of records. Past 256 routine names or 256 relations, the group keeps folding, the names beyond the bound are not kept, and the folded event gets `*` and the always-reported mark (loud, not ambiguous).
3. **Ending a group without folding** (R4's cases, a bound of item 4, or a timeout of item 5): the group's block-ful part becomes **one** event of its own at the group's first position, with its relations, counters and largest row count; its block-less part keeps H1 fix 2: ambiguous, with a window from the **first block-less position** (R14 item 4). One event per group replaces R4's event per record; `volume.large_result` is unaffected, since it reads row counts, which routine records do not have (R14), apart from selectable procedures, whose largest count is kept.
4. **One budget per stream for all pending groups** (security review M1): at most **4 096 pending groups** and **16 MiB** of group state in all, and at most **16 pending transactions per binding**. A group that would pass a bound ends the **oldest** pending group of the stream (for the per-binding bound, the binding's oldest) as item 3 says, fail closed, until the new group fits. A forger that opens many transactions with nested records can therefore end real groups early: their reads are still reported (block-ful part as its own event; block-less part ambiguous, loud), never hidden.
5. **Block-ful-only groups end after 10 s of reading time** (R1 rule 7's count) without their statement finish, as item 3 says: a block-ful group that is not folded opens no window, so it needs no longer wait.
6. **The hold of other bindings' records** is needed only while a group holds a **block-less** record (it may open a window that covers them). While one pends, records of other bindings are held **in parsed form** (their buffer zeroized after parsing; kept: principal, binding, objects from the text and from the block kept apart, counters, row counts, signals, the undeclared-database mark, the own-record decision), so that a window opened later can still apply H1 fix 3 to them (objects from the counters plus `*`, never own, never dropped as a record of an undeclared database). Bounds, replacing R8 item 2's 256 records and 1 MiB: **65 536 held records, 16 MiB of parsed state per stream** (separate from item 4's budget), and 10 s of reading time (unchanged). Past a bound, the oldest group with a block-less record ends as item 3 says (a window from its first block-less position, the held records inside it).
7. **Persistence and re-reads** (security review M1, re-review M3 and L8).
   - **The held-back position.** The persisted read position does not pass the first block-less position of the oldest group with a block-less record, nor the `ATTACH_DATABASE` record of any external-source binding whose events are held (R29 item 2). A restart therefore re-reads every record whose outcome was not decided.
   - **A state checkpoint at that position.** With the position, the agent persists a **checkpoint of the parser state as it was at that position**: bindings, run identity, watermarks, credits, follow-ups, poisoning and taint, open windows, and the pending groups that existed there (block-ful-only groups included). Held external-source events are **not** in it (third-pass N6): their records lie after the checkpoint position, so the re-read regenerates them. **Inputs that do not come from the log** (third-pass L10) are persisted with the checkpoint, each with the log position at which it was taken ("Log positions", refinement of 2026-10-26): the execution credits issued up to the time of persisting (the re-read consumes them again), their follow-ups, the process start-time and `databases.conf` samples (R5, R11 12c), and the results of `check()` (the run confirmation, confirmed restores, the external-file, block-blind and view-dependency sets of R12 and R28, the linger of R30). The re-read applies each of them where it falls in the log, as the first reading did. A restart restores the checkpoint and re-reads from the position, so every state change of the re-read records (a binding created or ended, a credit or follow-up consumed, a binding poisoned) is applied again from the same starting state, and none is applied twice. Without a held-back position, the checkpoint is taken at the read position, as R1 rule 11's persisted state is today.
   - **Deduplication by position** then concerns only the events already sent: the persisted state keeps the positions (file identity and offset) of the records past the held-back position whose events were emitted, and a re-read record at such a position emits nothing again. Its state changes are still applied (from the checkpoint). Past the bound of item 9, re-emitted events are sent again, an accepted over-reporting (duplicates, never losses), counted in the local detail.
   - **Durability order** (third-pass L11). A deduplication position is recorded only after every event of its record is durably in the spool, and it is recorded **atomically per record position**: either all the events of that record are marked sent, or none is. Shedding (item 9) acts on the live state and sends its outputs (the ended group's event, the ambiguous records of a shed binding); a checkpoint that no longer holds the shed group or binding is persisted only after those outputs are durably spooled. So a crash at any point re-reads into a state that either still holds what was shed or has its outputs already sent.
8. **The bounds are checked** in the P10-D load run with per-row statements under concurrent sessions and many open transactions (measurement 3m); if they are exceeded, they are raised in an implementation note before the Audit lot ships, never removed.
9. **R1 rule 11's persisted list and cap, extended** (security review L8). The persisted state of R1 rule 11 gains the checkpoint of item 7 (with its pending groups and held external-source events) and the deduplication positions, under **one combined cap**: R1 rule 11's 16 MiB global cap covers all of it. When the cap would be passed, the agent fails closed in this order: the oldest pending groups end as item 3 says, external-source bindings with held events are shed (their records become ambiguous, R29 item 2, so the held-back position can move past their `ATTACH_DATABASE`), then deduplication positions are dropped (re-emitted events become accepted over-reporting). Held events themselves are not persisted (item 7, N6): they count toward R29's in-memory bounds, not this cap. If the state still exceeds the cap after these three steps, R1 rule 11's rule applies (a new binding is not kept, and its records are ambiguous), and a checkpoint is **never written truncated** (third-pass L11): the last complete checkpoint stays until a complete one fits. The first version of this refinement said that R1 rule 11 "already persists" the group state; it did not, and this item replaces that claim.
**Alternatives considered.** (a) Rely on the statement's block, which holds the sum: a forged statement finish could then drop the nested relations (N-1). (b) Per-binding queues instead of a global hold: the window that a non-folded block-less record opens applies to every binding's records after its position, so they must be held anyway. (c) A time bound alone: unbounded memory under a burst. (d) Keep raw records: 203 records already took 139 KiB; parameters would stay in memory longer. (e) A per-group budget only (the first version): many small groups could still exhaust memory (M1).

**Residual risks.** A per-row statement that runs longer than 10 s of reading time with a block-less routine, or behind which more than 65 536 records of other bindings are written, is ambiguous (loud, capping the level). A client that opens more than 16 transactions with pending nested records on one binding, or many such groups, ends groups early (item 4): loud or reported, never hidden.

**Effect.** No change to levels, notes or protocol.

### R26. DDL trigger name lines (measurement 3g)

**Measured**: DDL trigger records are written before their DDL statement's finish record, on the same attachment and transaction, with R14's routine trailer (the performance line, a block when the trigger touched records, no `records fetched`). Name lines on 5.0.4: `Trigger DDL_BEFORE_ANY (BEFORE DDL):`, `Trigger DDL_AFTER_DT (AFTER DDL):` and `Trigger DDL_AFTER_CT FOR DDL_LOG (AFTER DDL):`, where ` FOR <relation>` names a table the trigger's body wrote to (a DDL trigger belongs to no table); 4.0.7 the tab form of 3e (`\tDDL_AFTER_CT FOR DDL_LOG (AFTER DDL) `). The DDL statement's block includes the triggers' relations with the system relations it changed.

**Proposed**, amending R14 item 2's closed kind list: it gains `BEFORE DDL` and `AFTER DDL`, for which ` FOR <relation>` is optional. The right-anchored parse (R14 item 2, N4) reads the kind, then an optional ` FOR <relation>`, then the routine name. Neither name is ever an object (objects come only from the block, the routine name only as R4's folded name), so a name that holds ` FOR ` changes no object. DDL trigger records follow R14's routine rules and fold into their DDL statement, as R15 item 1 said (confirmed); the DDL statement keeps its kind only (decision 6), and its block's user relations are reported, system relations outside the always-reported lists being noise. A kind outside the list stays ambiguous (R14 item 2).

**Effect.** No change to levels, notes or protocol.

### R27. `gbak` backups, utility attachments and BLR requests (measurement 3g)

**Measured**: with `log_blr_requests = true`, `gbak -b` over TCP, through `service_mgr` and embedded wrote **no BLR record**. Its attachments wrote only `TRACE_INIT`, `ATTACH_DATABASE`, `START_TRANSACTION`, `COMMIT_TRANSACTION` (a performance line, no block), `DETACH_DATABASE` and `TRACE_FINI`, although the backup read every table; the client line of the TCP attachments is `gbak:<pid>`, which the client sets. The services log had `ATTACH_SERVICE`, `START_SERVICE` (`"Backup Database"` and its arguments) and `DETACH_SERVICE`. The embedded `gbak` wrote the same shape under its own pid, with the attachment line `(ATT_<n>, SYSDBA:NONE, NONE, <internal>)`. The trailer of BLR finish records could not be measured: none of the tools tried writes one. Not measured: whether a BLR client other than `gbak` is traced, what an attachment of `service_mgr` looks like, and whether the transaction-end performance line of a `gbak` transaction shows its page fetches.

The first version of this refinement proposed to accept, as a residual, that reads through BLR requests might not be reported at all. The security review (H1) found that this pre-accepted an unmeasured gap that may be open to an ordinary account. The proposal is split accordingly.

**Proposed**:

1. **Detection through `signature.backup_database`**, amending the `gbak` branch of decision 6, "Signals" ("BLR requests reading a whole relation from an attachment whose process name is `gbak`"), which cannot hold since no BLR record is written. The signal is raised, with an event naming `*` of the database, the always-reported mark and the record's principal, by:
   - (a) a well-formed `START_SERVICE` record of a service binding (R1 rule 6) whose service name is `Backup Database` or `Incremental Backup Database` (a closed list). The database argument is resolved under R2, then discarded, as attachment paths are; the other arguments are dropped at parse (decision 6). A name that resolves to a declared database gives the event for it; a name that resolves to a **known undeclared** database (an absolute path or a mapped alias that is not a declared database's) is counted and dropped (I5; security review N2); a name that does not resolve is attributed to every declared database of the target (H1 fix 3's attribution). This is the server's own record of the backup;
   - (b) an `ATTACH_DATABASE` of a declared database whose client process line names `gbak` (the last path component before `:<pid>` equal to `gbak` or `gbak.exe`), compared at parse and then dropped like every client line (never kept or sent). The client sets that line, so (b) is a hint that catches a `gbak` run over TCP, not a proof.

   One backup can raise both (a) and (b): two events, accepted as noise. An embedded `gbak` writes under its own pid, so R1 rule 2 makes its records ambiguous (always reported, with `*`, after one stop and a failed confirmation; the R5 residual).
2. **The services section is required for Partial**, amending proof 6 (security review H1): the services section must have `enabled = true` and `log_services = true` and write the declared log. A missing services section, or one that writes another file or lacks `log_services`, gives `audit.trace_settings_missing` with the label `log_services` (already in the unreleased revision `target_status.note_labels.2026_10_d`) and caps the level at **Limited**; `audit.services_not_audited` is reported as before. **Proof 11 is amended** to say so: a services section whose `log_filename` is the declared log is allowed, since service records are bound as service sessions (R1 rule 6) and their database arguments are resolved as item 1 (a) says; the default database section rule and the named database sections are unchanged.
3. **(i) The utility residual, for the maintainer** (open question 6). A `gbak` backup is a full read of the database, and it is reported only as a backup (the signal, with `*`), never with the tables read or a row count. Its scope is the accounts that can run a utility attachment that reads the whole database: measurement 3j records, on 4.0.7 and 5.0.4, which privileges that takes (the database owner, `RDB$ADMIN`, the 4.0+ system privilege `USE_GBAK_UTILITY`, or any account). docs/08 states the residual with that scope.
4. **(ii) BLR clients of ordinary accounts** (open question 7). If 3j shows that an **ordinary account** (not the owner, without `RDB$ADMIN` and without the backup system privilege) can read a table through BLR requests with no record that names the read, the **Partial level is withheld** (Limited, with the label of "Protocol" below) on every Firebird target, and docs/08 states that BLR reads are not seen. No counter-based rule restores Partial in this refinement (item 5). If 3j shows that such reads are traced (a BLR record with a trailer), they follow H1 fix 2 and R14 item 5, and nothing more is needed.
5. **An extra event from the transaction-end counters, never a condition for Partial** (re-review H2). 3j also measures whether the `COMMIT_TRANSACTION` or `ROLLBACK_TRANSACTION` performance line of a `gbak` or BLR transaction shows its page fetches (`fetch(es)`, R31). If it does, a transaction of a user binding (not internal, sweep or the agent's own) **with no statement, routine or BLR record** whose end record shows `fetch(es)` above a small bound (set by 3j from the fetches of an empty transaction, with a margin) gives an extra always-reported event naming `*` of the database, under the binding's principal, with no row count. That event can only over-report. It is **not** a detection that keeps Partial, because an ordinary account can bypass it:
   - **scenario 1**: the client adds any SQL statement to its BLR transaction, so "no statement record" no longer holds and no event is raised;
   - **scenario 2**: comparing the end record's fetches with the sum of the transaction's statement fetches does not help, because a client can end its own statement text with a fake trailer whose performance line claims any number of fetches, which inflates the sum.

   The review also found that the transaction-end counters themselves cannot be split away by an ordinary client: the server writes them after everything the client controls in that record, so the end record's own count is reliable; what fails is attributing that count to reads that no other record shows. A counter-based detection may restore Partial only through a **later refinement, with its own security review, after 3j**, that covers both scenarios.
6. **BLR finish records keep H1 fix 2** as written (`records fetched` expected; R14 item 5 unchanged), and proof 4 keeps `log_blr_requests = true`: it costs nothing and covers whatever the server writes.
7. **Embedded `gbak` and the user guide** (security review N3). The user guide says that a scheduled embedded `gbak` (run on the server host as root or as the server's account, with write access to the log) writes records under its own pid: each run costs one stop and makes its records ambiguous, capping the Partial level for 24 h (proof 8). Backups should go through the services manager or over loopback TCP.

**Alternatives considered.** (a) Pre-accept untraced BLR reads as a residual (the first version): rejected by the review (H1), since the gap may be open to any account. (b) Raise the signal from (b) alone: a backup through the services manager may carry another client line. (c) Report the `gbak` attachment's reads from the catalog (every table): the agent would invent objects the log does not show. (d) Withhold Partial for (i) too: utility backups are visible as backups through the server's own `START_SERVICE` record, now required.

**Effect.** The services section becomes a Partial requirement (label `log_services`, existing). One registry text in `signals.json` changes ("Protocol" below). If item 4 applies, Firebird Audit is Limited at best, with a reason to report (a label, in the same contract pull request); item 5 does not change that.

### R28. External-file tables, global temporary tables and views over virtual relations (measurement 3h)

**Measured**: external-file tables **never write a block**, even when a read returns rows (`SELECT A FROM EXT_T`: 3 rows, no block); an aggregate over one returns 1 row with no block. Global temporary tables write blocks as persistent tables do (`GTT_P` 3 natural, `GTT_D` 2 natural) and none when empty. Reads behind a view of a virtual relation write no block of it (`V_MON`, `V_SECU`, `V_CFG`: `V_CFG` returned 77 rows on 5.0.4, 73 on 4.0.7, with no block); a view that mixes virtual and persistent relations names only the persistent ones. R12 item 4's premise ("a statement that returned rows from a persistent table … touched records, so its block is missing") does not hold for external-file tables, and each such read opens a split window.

**Proposed**, amending R12 items 1 and 4:

1. **External-file tables are block-blind.** A block-less statement finish whose text parses (R8 item 1), calls no code (R8 case (c) otherwise), names at least one external-file table (`RDB$EXTERNAL_FILE` set, from the agent's catalog read of that database: the `RELATIONS` text of decision 5 already reads that boolean), and names no relation other than external-file tables and virtual relations, is **well formed whatever its `records fetched`** (no split window). It gives an event of its principal naming the external-file tables (and the virtual relations under R8's filters), with `rows` set to `records fetched`, `signature.remote_data_access`, and `*` with the always-reported mark, as decision 6 already says for reads of external-file tables. A text that also names a persistent table, a global temporary table or a view keeps R12 item 4 (a block-ful record is judged as usual; a block-less one with rows is ambiguous). A relation that the agent's latest catalog read does not know is not an external-file table (fail closed: a table created between two re-evaluations is ambiguous until the next one).
2. **Global temporary tables** (relation types 4 and 5) join R12 item 1's `n = 1` aggregate branch, next to type 0: they write blocks when touched. Views and external-file tables stay out of it.
3. **Views over virtual relations only.** From the bounded view-dependency read of R12 item 5, `check()` also computes the views that are **block-blind**: every base relation, transitively, is a virtual relation, and their transitive `RDB$DEPENDENCIES` hold **no procedure, function, UDR or package** (security review L4: a view can call code, whose reads follow other rules). A block-less record whose text names only block-blind views, virtual relations and external-file tables is well formed whatever its `records fetched` (R8 case (a), with the view as the object, and `*` with the always-reported mark when the view depends on an always-reported relation, R12 item 5). A view that also reads a persistent relation, or depends on code, is not block-blind. A cut or failed dependency read makes no view block-blind (fail closed), while R12 item 5 still counts every view as dependent.
4. **View DDL invalidates the sets** (security review L4). Any DDL statement record on a declared database that creates, alters, recreates or drops a view (`CREATE`, `ALTER`, `CREATE OR ALTER`, `RECREATE`, `DROP VIEW`; recognised from the statement kind) invalidates that database's block-blind set and R12 item 5's dependency set from that record's position until the next `check()`: in between, no view is block-blind and every view counts as dependent. Such a record asks for an early `check()`, merged under R11 8d's limit (at most one per 30 s per target, never while a check runs). A DDL text that does not parse, or a DDL record that is ambiguous, invalidates the sets the same way.

**Forging.** A forger can end its own record with a fake block-less trailer on a text naming an external-file table or a block-blind view: this gives nothing beyond R12's fake trailer; the real trailer lands in the forged record that follows, judged by R1 rules 3, 4 and 8. A forged view DDL record only invalidates the sets (more ambiguity, loud) and costs at most one extra check per 30 s.

**Alternatives considered.** (a) Keep R12 item 4 for these relations: one split window per read of an external-file table or of a monitoring view with rows. (b) Treat every view as block-blind: a view over a persistent table that returned rows without a block would hide that its block moved.

**Residual risks.** Reads of external-file tables and of block-blind views are reported from the text, with the row count; the server gives no counters for them. A view over an external-file table was not measured and stays under R12 item 4 (ambiguous with rows). A view changed through a path that writes no DDL record in the declared log is caught at the next `check()`.

**Effect.** No change to levels, notes or protocol; `signature.remote_data_access` already lists `firebird`.

### R29. Local external data sources (measurement 7d)

**Measured**: a local external data source (`EXECUTE STATEMENT … ON EXTERNAL '<path>'` with no host) writes, on one token of the server process, `TRACE_INIT`, **`ATTACH_DATABASE`**, `START_TRANSACTION`, one `EXECUTE_STATEMENT_FINISH` per inner statement (text, `records fetched`, a block), the transaction-end record, `DETACH_DATABASE` and `TRACE_FINI`, in every configuration measured. The attachment line is `<path> (ATT_<n>, <user>:<role>, NONE, <internal>)`: the caller's user and `NONE` without `AS USER`, otherwise the `AS USER` name and the `ROLE`; no client line; a fresh id from the target database's counter. One attachment serves every inner statement of one caller transaction on one target; its transaction ends with the caller's, then it detaches. The inner attachment's records come before the caller's routine and statement finish records. A routine can open the declared database itself this way. An embedded local process (the embedded `gbak` of 3g) writes the same line, with no client line, under **its own pid**. The attachment line of `service_mgr`'s own database attachments was not recorded (measurement 3j).

**Proposed**, settling R18 item 3. The security review (M2) required that such a binding be no quieter than R18 item 3 as accepted (where every such record is ambiguous):

1. **The external-source binding.** An `ATTACH_DATABASE` whose attachment line is exactly `<name> (ATT_<n>, <user>:<role>, NONE, <internal>)` (a user and a role part, the character set field `NONE`, the protocol field exactly `<internal>`, no address, no port), with no client line, **on the current run's pid**, anchors a binding under R1 rules 3 to 5 and R7 (fresh id, fresh token, the watermark, the order rule): an **external-source binding**. Its principal is the user and role of that line. R1 rule 9 does not apply (no address). It is never the agent's own (the agent sends no PSQL, and the own rule requires the agent's attachment ids and local ports), and never an internal binding (R16: the Garbage Collector only).
2. **A caller is required** (M2). The external-source binding's records are judged as any binding's (statement texts through the normalizer, parameter lines dropped, R12 for the no-record shape), but its events are **held** (in parsed form) until its transaction-end record, within their own bounds (security review L9): at most **10 minutes of reading time** per binding (R4's bound for a nested record with no statement finish), and at most 16 384 held events and 4 MiB per stream, in memory (they are not persisted, R25 item 7); the persisted position is held back at its `ATTACH_DATABASE` meanwhile (R25 item 7). Then, where the parser checks that a **caller** exists: another live binding of the same run (same pid) whose transaction was started before the external binding's `ATTACH_DATABASE`, is still open at that `ATTACH_DATABASE`, and has written, between that `ATTACH_DATABASE` and the external binding's transaction end, a finish record of **code** (R8 case (c): `EXECUTE STATEMENT … ON EXTERNAL`, a procedure, a function outside the built-in list, `EXECUTE BLOCK`, or a routine finish record). With a caller, the held events are emitted, under the external binding's principal. Without one (none found, the caller's database not in the declared log, a hold bound reached, or a held binding shed under R25 item 9), every record of the external-source binding is **ambiguous** (always reported, with `*`), as R18 item 3 made them. docs/08 states that such reads appear as another attachment with no address, under the `ON EXTERNAL` user or the caller's, next to the calling statement's always-reported `*` event with `signature.remote_data_access` (R18).
3. **Transactions with no statement record.** A transaction of an external-source binding that writes no statement record but whose end record shows page fetches above R27 item 5's bound (if 3j shows the counters) gives the extra always-reported `*` event of R27 item 5, as for every user binding. As there (re-review H2), it can only over-report and is no condition for any level.
4. **The embedded local process.** It writes the same line under another pid. R1 rule 2 then applies: a stop, a confirmation that fails (`MON$SERVER_PID` is the server's), the pid remembered, and every record of that pid ambiguous (always reported, with `*`). The external-source binding exists only on the run's pid, so the two cannot be confused, and both cases fail closed. Processes that run the embedded engine with write access to the log are outside what the parser defends against (R5 residual, proof 1).
5. **What this changes for a forger, and the R1 forger analysis** (M2). Any client can write this line (8f). A forger that runs a code statement in its own open transaction (any procedure call) meets the caller condition of item 2 with its own real binding, so a forged `<internal>` `ATTACH_DATABASE` with a user is a fresh anchored binding under which a captured real trailer is reported: the accepted misattribution residual, with the real objects and row count. Unlike a TCP line, it needs no held socket. So **R1 rule 9 no longer raises the cost of misattribution**: the R1 forger analysis ("Rule 9, if measurement 8f allows it, makes this harder (a live socket with no other binding is needed)") and the restated residual are amended to say that a forger who can call code avoids rule 9 by forging an `<internal>` line. Rule 9 still makes a forged TCP line with a wrong or reused 4-tuple ambiguous, at little cost. Open question 7 of the refinement of 2026-10-25 (apply rule 9) and open question 9 below are put to the maintainer together.

**Alternatives considered.** (a) Keep these attachments ambiguous (R18 item 3 as accepted): every use of a local external data source on a declared database caps the level for 24 h, and rule 9 keeps its cost. (b) Bind without a caller (the first version): quieter than the accepted text (M2). (c) Tie the binding to one caller attachment: the records carry no reference between the two, so only the weaker "a live caller with a code record" can be checked.

**Residual risks** (for docs/08):
- Next to the misattribution residual: misattribution through a forged `<internal>` attachment line needs a code call in the forger's own transaction but no held socket; the read stays reported with its real objects and row count.
- **Long caller transactions** (security review L9): a caller transaction that uses a local external data source and lasts longer than 10 minutes of reading time, or more held events than item 2's bounds, makes the external-source binding's records ambiguous (always reported, loud, capping the level for 24 h). Measured in 3m.
- **A held-back position** (third-pass N7): a forged external-source `ATTACH_DATABASE` holds the persisted read position back at its record for up to 10 minutes of reading time, and a forger can repeat it on a rolling basis. The cost is a longer re-read and duplicate events after a restart (deduplicated within R25 item 9's cap, over-reported beyond it), never a hidden read.

**Effect.** No change to levels, notes or protocol.

### R30. The Garbage Collector's end, and the lowest-id condition (measurement 8i)

**Measured** (SuperServer):
- Every Garbage Collector token wrote `TRACE_INIT`, `START_TRANSACTION`, `COMMIT_TRANSACTION`, `TRACE_FINI` and nothing else (69 bindings); its id was the opener's id plus 2 in every sequential open.
- **Without a linger**, its `COMMIT_TRANSACTION`, then its `TRACE_FINI`, came 0 to 4 ms after the open's last user `DETACH_DATABASE`, with no user binding live, in all 61 cases. **With `LINGER TO 15`**, 15.000 to 15.001 s after the last user detach (8 of 8); a session that attached inside the linger window did not start a second Garbage Collector, and the first committed 15 s after that later detach.
- R16 item 2's lowest-id condition (above the lowest attachment id read in the current open) held for 34 of 34 real Garbage Collectors on 4.0.7 and rejected **one of 35** on 5.0.4 (1 of 69 in all): out-of-order attach records let every user binding the parser knew detach while the database was still open, so a new open began whose lowest id read was above the Garbage Collector's.

**Proposed**, amending R16 items 2 and 3:

1. **The lowest-id condition is dropped**, and with it R16 item 3 (the open), which only that condition used. The condition rejects real Garbage Collectors (loud, capping the level for 24 h) and protects no read: a forged internal binding carries none (R6, R16), and the unseen ids below an open's lowest id are those of earlier opens' Cache Writers, which write nothing. The other conditions of R16 item 2 stay: an unseen id, the run bound, the seen-id floor, one live internal binding per database identity with the replacement rule, one transaction.
2. **The end is bound to the database close.** The internal binding's `COMMIT_TRANSACTION` and `TRACE_FINI` are well formed only when **no other binding of its database identity is live** when they are read (61 of 61 without a linger, 8 of 8 with one). Otherwise they are ambiguous and poison the internal binding. This also removes most of R16's forging case: a forged `COMMIT_TRANSACTION` written from a client's statement record is written while that client's own binding is live, so it is ambiguous.
3. **The expected end.** After the last other binding of the identity ends, the internal binding is expected to end within 10 s of reading time, or, when the database's linger L is known and above 0, within L + 10 s; the clock restarts whenever another binding of the identity is created. Past that, the internal binding ends (a later record of it is ambiguous) and the slot is free. L is read by `check()` from `RDB$DATABASE.RDB$LINGER` (a new constant text of `sql::STATEMENTS` with its 3d row, R20, R23; its visibility to the minimal account is measured with 3l). When L is not known (not read, failed, not visible), no end bound applies and the replacement rule alone frees the slot, as now.

**Alternatives considered.** (a) Keep the lowest-id condition: about 1 in 69 real Garbage Collectors on 5.0.4 caps the level for 24 h. (b) Keep it and repair the open with a time margin before an open ends: the margin would depend on R7's out-of-order bounds and still guard no read. (c) Bind the end without reading the linger: under a linger, every real Garbage Collector would outlive a 10 s bound and be ambiguous.

**Residual risks** (restating R16's, for docs/08):
- A forged internal binding still carries no read. It can hold its database's internal slot until the expected end of item 3, or, without a known linger, until the replacement rule frees it; a forged `COMMIT_TRANSACTION` or `TRACE_FINI` that passes item 2 can only be written when no other binding of that database is live (for example from client-set text in the record that ends the last binding, if that record carries any), and there it captures no trailer.
- **A forged binding that never detaches** (security review L5): a forged `ATTACH_DATABASE` with no `DETACH_DATABASE` is an anchored binding of a declared database, never evicted (R1 rule 11). While it lives, item 2's "no other binding live" never holds, so the real Garbage Collector's end is ambiguous **at every close of that database for the rest of the run**: a durable, loud denial of the Partial level (each such record counted in `audit.log_records_ambiguous`), in the same class as R1 rule 11's durable-denial residual. It hides no read. The docs/08 follow-up (P10-G) states it next to that residual.

**Effect.** No change to levels, notes or protocol. One new constant text (`RDB$LINGER`), agent side.

### R31. Confirmations and smaller facts

- **The performance line** (security review N4) of statement, routine and transaction-end records often carries page counters after the time (`0 ms, 1 read(s), 14 fetch(es), 1 mark(s)`, also `write(s)`). Its grammar, amending H1 fix 1, is exact: the leading spaces of the version (from the matrix table), a decimal number of at most 20 digits, ` ms`, then zero or more counters from the closed list `read(s)`, `write(s)`, `fetch(es)`, `mark(s)`, each at most once and in that order, each written `, <decimal> <name>`, then the end of the line. The order and the list are kept per major series (4.0.x, 5.0.x) and checked by a drift test on the engine matrix, as R14 item 2's name lines; a line with anything else is not a performance line, so the record has no trailer (H1 fix 2), and a grammar change in a later patch release is loud.
- **Proof 7 confirmed**: the read-back probe's record is the exact text, `1 records fetched` and a block naming `RDB$DATABASE`, for every account and position.
- **R15 confirmed** on 416 database-trigger records over both versions and modes, every one meeting R15 item 2's conditions; `ON CONNECT` comes **after** its attachment's `ATTACH_DATABASE`, which removes R15's second residual (the place of `ON CONNECT` records). A block-ful database-trigger record is a read by the attachment's principal, on the agent's attachments too (R15 item 4).
- **R12 confirmed**: an aggregate over a view of a virtual relation or over an external-file table returns 1 row with no block, so R12 item 1 rightly keeps views and external-file tables out of the `n = 1` branch (R28 item 2 admits only global temporary tables); reads behind views of virtual relations write no block (R12 item 5's premise).
- **R14 confirmed** for per-row and DDL triggers (trailers without `records fetched`, folding into their statement, innermost first).
- **R16 item 1 confirmed** (the record sequence and the opener-plus-2 id in sequential opens; no Garbage Collector in SuperClassic).

### Obligations still open after this refinement

Each is measured before the pull request that relies on it; 3i, 3j and 3k before the Audit lot (lot 3) merges:

- **3i** (R22, connector lot): the records the server writes when the connector closes a cursor at the client deadline between rounds, when it cancels a pending fetch, and for a stale cancel (item 3), on 4.0.7 and 5.0.4, with the exact shape of each follow-up `ERROR` record per version (item 7); the conditions of the `request synchronization error` after a row-limit cut; the delay between a dropped attachment and the server's records of it (N5); recorded in the own-statement table.
- **3j** (R27, R29), on 4.0.7 and 5.0.4, from a throwaway test harness (never the connector, whose operation set excludes BLR compilation, decision 1):
  - a BLR request that reads a table, sent by an **ordinary account** (not the owner, no `RDB$ADMIN`, no backup system privilege) with an arbitrary process name, with `log_blr_requests = true`: whether any record names the read, and its trailer;
  - the same client setting the utility (backup) attachment tag in its DPB without the `gbak` process name: what the server allows and writes;
  - the privileges a utility attachment that reads the whole database needs (the owner, `RDB$ADMIN`, `USE_GBAK_UTILITY`);
  - whether the `COMMIT_TRANSACTION` / `ROLLBACK_TRANSACTION` performance line of a `gbak` or BLR transaction shows its page fetches, and the fetches of an empty transaction (R27 item 5's bound);
  - the attachment line of `service_mgr`'s database attachments during a services backup (R29).
- **3k** (R24): that user DML on system tables is refused on both versions (a matrix test); `ALTER SESSION RESET` on a database with connect and disconnect triggers; whether an API commit's `COMMIT_TRANSACTION` carries a commit trigger's block.
- **3l** (R30): `RDB$DATABASE.RDB$LINGER` readable by the minimal account, and the expected-end rule on the 8i scenarios.
- **3m** (R25), in the P10-D load run with **8g** (R7, unchanged): per-row statements under concurrent sessions within the hold bounds (65 536 records, 16 MiB, 10 s), many open transactions with pending groups within the group budget (4 096 groups, 16 MiB, 16 transactions per binding), the 10 s end of block-ful-only groups, a restart during a pending group and **a restart while an external-source binding's events are held** (the checkpoint, the held-back position and deduplication, R25 item 7, R29 item 2), and long caller transactions with a local external data source (R29's 10-minute hold).
- Still open from earlier: `PLG$USERS` in a security database built with the legacy schema, Classic under socket activation, the `sysdba_mapped` account on the matrix (obligation 15).

### Protocol (P10-C)

**One registry text change** (security review L6), in a P10-C pull request with the `security-reviewer` review that every `shared/protocol/` change requires (AGENTS.md): the Firebird part of the description of `signature.backup_database` in `shared/protocol/signals.json`, which names "BLR requests reading a whole relation from a client whose process name is gbak", becomes "a services Backup Database or Incremental Backup Database, a connection whose client process name is gbak (declared by the client, so evadable), ALTER DATABASE BEGIN BACKUP (nbackup), CREATE SHADOW, ALTER DATABASE ADD FILE or ADD DIFFERENCE FILE" (R27 item 1). `firebird` is already among its engines and no released console lists `engine.firebird`. **If R27 item 4 applies** (3j shows untraced BLR reads by an ordinary account; R27 item 5 does not change that), the reason for the Limited level needs a `TargetNoteLabel` (for example `blr_reads_not_traced` for `audit.trace_settings_missing` or another existing code), added to the unreleased revision `target_status.note_labels.2026_10_d` in the same pull request; that is decided once 3j is measured. Nothing else: the label `log_services` of R27 item 2 is already in that revision; the credit bounds, the error follow-ups, the per-text metadata-loading sets, the transaction blocks, the pending groups and the hold bounds, the DDL trigger kinds, the external-source binding, the block-blind relations and the Garbage Collector's end are parser, `check()` and connector rules of the agent, with no new note code, field, signal, capability token or metric. Events of transaction records and of not-folded routine groups leave out the optional `rows`, as database-trigger and sweep events do (refinement b, "Protocol"). The Audit lot keeps its `security-reviewer` review (AGENTS.md).

Review of the first version of this refinement (`06ae2e9`); verdict **not ready**, one High, two Mediums, six Lows and five notes. Every finding is applied in this revision; a re-review is requested.

### Open questions (for the maintainer, refinement of 2026-10-26 c)

Each with the answer this refinement proposed, revised to the security review of 2026-10-26 (c), its re-review and its third pass. **Decided by the maintainer on 2026-10-27: every proposed answer accepted**, question 6 (the utility residual) and question 9 with open question 7 of the refinement of 2026-10-25 included.

1. **R22**: credit the agent's own reads by the rows requested (requested ≤ `records fetched` ≤ requested + the largest request after an early close; the last request subtracted from the lower bound when the attachment was lost), and change the connector so that a client deadline in a multi-round fetch closes the cursor between rounds, or cancels a pending fetch and waits for its answer (a stale cancel expected; after a stale cancel whose `DSQL_drop` succeeds, the session ended cleanly until 3i decides), instead of dropping the attachment?
   **Proposed**: yes. The connector knows what it requested; the change makes the record arrive at the deadline.
2. **R22 items 4 to 8**: own `FAILED` finishes only for credits ended by the server's statement timeout, one exact-shape error follow-up per credit (timeout, synchronization error, stale cancel), any other server error not own?
   **Proposed**: yes. Follow-up records carry no object, and a forged or extra one is ambiguous.
3. **R23**: per-text metadata-loading sets and row-dependent must-read sets (a block or none with no counted row)?
   **Proposed**: yes.
4. **R24**: blocks on transaction-end records and transaction statements well formed, reported under the binding's principal, system relations outside the always-reported lists as noise?
   **Proposed**: yes, with 3k.
5. **R25**: pending groups with a per-relation maximum, one stream budget (4 096 groups, 16 MiB, 16 transactions per binding, ended oldest first), block-ful-only groups ended after 10 s, a hold of other bindings' records only while a block-less record pends (65 536 records, 16 MiB, 10 s), and at restart a parser-state checkpoint at the held-back position with deduplication of emitted events, all persisted under R1 rule 11's one 16 MiB cap?
   **Proposed**: yes, with 3m.
6. **R27 (i)**: accept, for docs/08, that a utility (`gbak`) backup is reported only as a backup (`signature.backup_database`, `*`), scoped to the accounts 3j shows can run one, with the services section required for Partial?
   **For the maintainer.** Proposed: accept. The server's own `START_SERVICE` record, now required, shows every services backup.
7. **R27 (ii)**: if 3j shows untraced BLR reads by an ordinary account, withhold Partial (Limited, with a label), with the transaction-end counter event of item 5 only as extra over-reporting, and any counter-based way back to Partial left to a later refinement with its own security review after 3j?
   **Proposed**: yes. A read open to any account and seen by no record is not compatible with Partial, and the counter rule is bypassable (H2).
8. **R26**: `BEFORE DDL` and `AFTER DDL`, with an optional ` FOR <relation>` that is never an object?
   **Proposed**: yes.
9. **R29, with open question 7 of the refinement of 2026-10-25**: bind local external data sources only with a live caller (same pid, an open transaction, a code record), their events held up to 10 minutes of reading time with the persisted position held back at their `ATTACH_DATABASE`, otherwise ambiguous (longer caller transactions a docs/08 residual); and accept that R1 rule 9 no longer raises the cost of misattribution, since a forger who can call code can forge an `<internal>` line?
   **For the maintainer, together.** Proposed: accept both; rule 9 stays, for the forged TCP lines it still catches. Rejecting keeps these attachments ambiguous (R18 item 3 as accepted), each use capping the level for 24 h, and rule 9 keeps its cost.
10. **R28**: external-file tables, and views over virtual relations only with no code dependency, block-blind; view DDL invalidating the sets until the next check; global temporary tables in R12's aggregate branch?
    **Proposed**: yes.
11. **R30**: drop the lowest-id condition, accept the Garbage Collector's end only with no other live binding of its database, end it within 10 s (or the linger plus 10 s) after the last one, with the never-detaching forged binding as a stated, loud residual?
    **Proposed**: yes.
12. **Protocol**: change the `signature.backup_database` registry text in a P10-C pull request with a `security-reviewer` review, and add a label only if question 7 applies?
    **Proposed**: yes.

## Refinement (2026-10-27, accepted by the maintainer on 2026-10-27): the fourth-round lot 3 measurements

Status: **Accepted by the maintainer on 2026-10-27 with the proposed answers** to every open question (1 to 6), including question 1 (Partial kept with the silent utility residual stated plainly in docs/08 and the user guide, its scope as written until 3n, the narrowed detection of R32 item 5 decided after 3n). **Proposed on 2026-10-27 and revised after its [security review of 2026-10-27](#security-review-of-2026-10-27-refinement-of-2026-10-27)** (verdict on the first version, `94d4133`: no Critical or High finding, ready once M1 and M2 are applied; every finding applied in this revision). It answers the [fourth round of lot 3 prerequisite measurements](../measurements/phase10-firebird-facts.md#lot-3-prerequisites-fourth-round-2026-10-27) (measurements 3i, 3j and 3k, which the accepted refinement of 2026-10-26 c lists under ["Obligations still open after this refinement"](#obligations-still-open-after-this-refinement-1); pull request #381, [summary](../measurements/phase10-firebird-facts.md#fourth-round-summary)), records what they confirm, and adds two lot 3 obligations from the reviews of the contract pull requests #377 and #380. It amends, by reference:

- the refinement of 2026-10-26 c: R22 items 4 and 6, R24 item 3, R27 items 3 to 6 (item 3 (i), the accepted utility residual, restated: it is silent, not reported as a backup), R29 item 3 and its "Protocol" paragraph (the conditional label);
- the refinement of 2026-10-26 b: R14 item 5 and R15 item 2;
- the refinement of 2026-10-26: R8 case (b) and R8 item 3;
- decision 6: "Missing trailers" (H1 fix 2, for BLR records), "Trailer first" (H1 fix 1, the expected shapes) and "Signals".

The accepted text and the four accepted refinements stay as they are; where this refinement differs from them, this refinement applies. **No protocol change in this refinement**; once it is accepted, one follow-up registry text change ("Protocol" below). ROADMAP: [phase 10](../ROADMAP.md#phase-10--engine-scope-wave-2-sqlite-firebird), P10-B.

### R32. BLR reads by ordinary accounts are traced; the utility attachment (measurement 3j)

**Measured** (4.0.7 and 5.0.4): a BLR request that scans a table, sent by an ordinary account (not the owner, no `RDB$ADMIN`, no `USE_GBAK_UTILITY`) with an arbitrary process name and `log_blr_requests = true`, writes `COMPILE_BLR` (a performance line, no block) then `EXECUTE_BLR` (a performance line and a block naming the scanned relation, no `records fetched`). An ordinary account that sets the utility (backup) attachment tag `isc_dpb_gbak_attach` is refused at attach (`USE_GBAK_UTILITY` missing). An account whose role grants `USE_GBAK_UTILITY` is allowed the attach, but its BLR `SELECT` still checks object rights (`UNAUTHORIZED COMPILE_BLR`). `SYSDBA` with the tag reads a table through BLR and its attachment writes **no BLR record**: only `ATTACH_DATABASE`, `START_TRANSACTION`, `COMMIT_TRANSACTION` and `DETACH_DATABASE`. The utility attachment, not the `gbak` binary, is what suppressed BLR records in 3g. Only the `gbak` tag was measured; the other utility tags (`gfix`, `gstat`) and DSQL statements under a utility attachment were not. A `gbak` or BLR transaction's `COMMIT_TRANSACTION` performance line shows the same counters as an empty transaction's (`1 fetch(es), 1 mark(s)`). A `service_mgr` backup's database attachment is an ordinary TCP line (`TCPv4:127.0.0.1/<port>`), with no client line.

**Proposed**:

1. **R27 item 4 (ii) does not apply.** An ordinary account cannot read a table through BLR without a record that names the read. The Partial level is **not** withheld, and no label is added (the conditional label of R27's "Protocol" paragraph is withdrawn). BLR records follow R33. Proof 4 keeps `log_blr_requests = true`: it is now what makes these reads visible, and stays a label of `audit.trace_settings_missing` (Limited when missing).
2. **The utility residual (R27 item 3 (i), accepted) is silent, and is restated** (security review M1 and M2). R27 item 3 described it as a backup "reported only as a backup". That holds for services backups, not for the residual: the utility attachment tag (`isc_dpb_gbak_attach`) suppresses BLR request tracing, and **any client can set it in its DPB over TCP, with any process name and no services call**. An account allowed the utility attachment can therefore read, through BLR, the relations its object rights and system privileges let it read (every relation for the owner and `RDB$ADMIN`; for a holder of `USE_GBAK_UTILITY` alone, the relations it has `SELECT` on) with **no event, no signal and no `*`**: no record names the read, and no `START_SERVICE` record exists, so R27 item 1 does not see it either.
   - **Scope, stated conservatively until 3n**: the database owner, `RDB$ADMIN` and the holders of **any utility system privilege** (`USE_GBAK_UTILITY`, `USE_GFIX_UTILITY`, `USE_GSTAT_UTILITY`, and any other the matrix lists). 3j measured the `gbak` tag only; 3n measures every utility tag with an ordinary account and with the matching privilege, for BLR and DSQL reads. If 3n shows DSQL statement records are also suppressed under a utility attachment, the residual covers DSQL reads too; until then, docs/08 says that it may.
   - **docs/08 and the user guide** state it plainly: the owner, `RDB$ADMIN` and holders of a utility system privilege are **untraced readers** within their object rights; grant those privileges only to accounts trusted to read without audit. A non-administrator database owner could not be isolated in this round (a plain user's `CREATE DATABASE` was refused); the owner case rests on documented behaviour and on `SYSDBA` as owner.
   - Partial is kept, as the maintainer accepted for R27 (i); open question 1 asks whether that acceptance stands now that the residual is known to be silent.
3. **R27 item 5 is dropped**, and with it R29 item 3, which applied it to external-source bindings. 3j shows that the transaction-end counters do not include a backup's or a BLR scan's page fetches: the end record of a `gbak` transaction shows the counters of an empty transaction, so no bound can separate them. The rule was already only over-reporting (re-review H2), and it would fire on nothing measured while adding per-transaction state and a bound with no measured basis. R27 item 5's sentence on a later counter-based way back to Partial is moot, since Partial is not withheld. Open question 2 offers keeping it instead.
4. **The `service_mgr` backup's database attachment** is an ordinary TCP binding (R1 rules 3 to 5 and rule 9), not an `<internal>` line, so R29 is not involved. Its records (`START_TRANSACTION`, `COMMIT_TRANSACTION` with a performance line and no block, `DETACH_DATABASE`) produce no event; the backup is reported through `START_SERVICE` (R27 item 1 (a)). The direct-TCP `gbak` client line `gbak:<pid>` (R27 item 1 (b)) is confirmed. Rule 9 needs a server-side socket for that attachment's 4-tuple in `/proc/net/tcp`; 3n or the connector lot checks that a `service_mgr` backup's loopback attachment has one (security review N7), since otherwise every services backup would be ambiguous.
5. **A narrowed detection, decided after 3n** (security review M1). If 3n shows that a utility attachment can be told apart (its `ATTACH_DATABASE` line, or its `MON$ATTACHMENTS` row, which the minimal account sees only for its own attachments), or if `check()` knows that the attachment's principal is the owner, holds `RDB$ADMIN` or holds a utility system privilege (from `RDB$ROLES.RDB$SYSTEM_PRIVILEGES` and the `RDB$ADMIN` grants in `RDB$USER_PRIVILEGES`, if the minimal account can read them, 3n), then a transaction with no statement, routine or BLR record on an attachment of such a principal, not explained by a `START_SERVICE` of the same principal, gives an always-reported `*` event under that principal. It would over-report idle administrator transactions, and would not show what was read. It is proposed as an option for the maintainer (open question 1), decided in a later refinement or implementation note after 3n.

**Alternatives considered.** (a) Keep R27 item 5: see item 3 and open question 2. (b) Report every user transaction with no statement record (a possible utility attachment) as an always-reported `*` event: any idle or transaction-only client would raise one; item 5 narrows it to principals that can use a utility attachment. (c) Withhold Partial for the silent residual: it is open only to the owner, `RDB$ADMIN` and utility privilege holders, which the maintainer accepted for R27 (i); open question 1.

**Effect.** No change to levels (Partial stays reachable), notes or protocol. docs/08 and the user guide state the silent residual and its scope.

### R33. `COMPILE_BLR` and `EXECUTE_BLR` record shapes (measurement 3j)

**Measured**: see R32. `EXECUTE_BLR` is written **at the start** of the request's execution, so the counters of its block are those at that point: `Natural 1` whether the request then reads 3 rows or 10 000. The relation the request scans is always named.

H1 fix 2 and R14 item 5 (and R27 item 6) expect `records fetched` on BLR finish records, so every `EXECUTE_BLR` record would be ambiguous.

**Proposed**, replacing R14 item 5 and R27 item 6, and amending H1 fixes 1 and 2:

1. **Well-formed shapes**, judged trailer first like every record (H1 fix 1), with the R31 performance-line grammar:
   - `COMPILE_BLR`: a performance line, **no block** and no `records fetched`;
   - `EXECUTE_BLR`: a performance line, then **a block or none**, and no `records fetched`.

   A `records fetched` line in either, a block on `COMPILE_BLR`, or a missing performance line does not fit: ambiguous (H1 fix 1 or fix 2), with a window at its position as for any record whose trailer is missing. The lot 3 engine-matrix test records both shapes per version and server mode (SuperServer and SuperClassic, 3n); a different shape in a later patch release is loud (ambiguous), never lost. **The `FAILED` and `UNAUTHORIZED` forms are pending 3n** (security review L2): until 3n records them, they are ambiguous (always reported, with `*`); the `ERROR` record that follows keeps its GDS codes and drops its texts (decision 6).
2. **What a well-formed `EXECUTE_BLR` becomes.** An access event of the binding's principal (the attachment's user and role) naming the relations of its block under the filters (system relations outside the always-reported lists are noise; always-reported relations keep their name and mark), **plus `*` of its database, always reported**. The block is written at the start of the request, so it cannot show what the request reads afterwards (a second loop, another relation), and the parser does not read BLR. **No `rows`**: the counters are those at the request start, not a count of rows read, and `records fetched` is absent, so no counter-derived count is used (it would understate every scan) and `volume.large_result` and `shape.full_table_read` cannot apply. No split window opens for a well-formed record. A block-less `EXECUTE_BLR` gives the same event with `*` only.
3. **`COMPILE_BLR`.** A well-formed `COMPILE_BLR` produces no event (compilation reads no row; the read is reported by its `EXECUTE_BLR`). Once 3n records the `UNAUTHORIZED` and `FAILED` forms (item 1), the proposed handling is an event naming `*` of its database, always reported, under its principal, with no window (a refused BLR read whose relations the record does not name), confirmed in an implementation note.
4. **Never the agent's own.** The connector sends no BLR (decision 1), so a BLR record on an agent attachment misses R8 item 4's closed statement set and is handled as any client's record (R8 item 5): reported, always reported with `*`.
5. **BLR text never kept (I2).** The statement area of a BLR record, and any BLR dump under `print_blr`, are dropped at parse (decision 6: BLR can hold literals), never kept, logged or sent, and zeroized with the record buffer. They are injection points as statement texts are (H1 fix 6), handled trailer first.
